TO  WHOM  IT  MAY  CONCERN: 

Notice  is  hereby  given  that  Sign 
4  Hoard  Oil  Company,  a  '  corporation 
organized  and  existing  under  and  by 
virtue  of  the  laws  of  the  State  of 
Kentucky,  has  been  dissolved  and  all ! 
persons  having  claims  against  said 
orporation  are  hereby  notified  *o  pre¬ 
sent  same  to  the  Treasurer  for  adjust¬ 
ment  within  sixty  days  from  this  date, 
and  all  personwowing  said  corporation 
will  call  and  pay  same.

DRV  CL6ANING 

Brushing  the  surface  of  garments  with  gasoline  will  not  clean  them, 
dy  so  doing,  the  dust,  grit  and  grime  are  merely  driven  into  the  lining 
and  inner  part  of  the  garment  and  will  re-appear  in  a  few  days. 

With  our  “I  aby  Grand”  Dry-Cleaning  plant  we  wash  the  garments  with 
•  gasoline  and  plenty  of  good  benzine  soap— said  soap^tiemg  Uf  -gaadfir.t* 
what  P-G  is  to  water— then  rinse  them  iu  good  clean  gasoline.  By  this 
method  w4  clean  linings  and  Hood  out  ail  dust  and  accumulated  dirt. 
Having  extracted  all  surplus  gasoline  from  garments,  we  dry  them  b\ 
our  Hot-Air  method,  leaving  them  free  from  any  odor  of  gasoline.  And 
last  they  are  pressed  by  hot  dry  steam,  which  penetrates  every  part  of 
the  garment,  raises  the  nap  and  puts  new  life  into  the  fabric. 

We  solicit  your  patronage.  Special  attention  to  out-of-town  orders 
Telephones:  Shop.  42  Residence.  51 -J

A  Road  from 
Alaska  to  Peru  -v\ 

Fr'w  people  rral:ze  flic  size  of  the 
Louisville  Ar  Nashvilk  Railroad 
bystem,  and  the  extent  to  which  its 
tracks  cover  and  serve  the  lieart  of 
the  Southland. 

The  L.  &  N.  operates  5,040  miles 
of  road,  to  winch  should  b..  added 
2,690  miles  of  double  and  sv\  itching  /A 
tracks,  making  a  total  of  7,730  % 
miles  m  all  .  \ 

If  all  of  the  tracks  operated  by  the  L.  & 

w,Zr(i  Ptacc(I  i®  a  continuoua  line,  they 
would  form  a  road  stretching  from  .Alaska, 
to  Peru  in  South  America;  or,  they  would 
lay  u  double  track  from  Boston  to  San 
Francisco,  with  some  to  spare. 

The  L  &  N.  enters  thirteen  states:  con¬ 
nects  more  than,  a  dozen  large  cities  and 
serves  a  vast  urban  and  rural  population.

WHAT  CAN  CHIROPRACTIC  DO  FOR  ME? 


The  answer  is  simple:  If  the  CAUSE  of  your  trouble  is  Nerve  Pres- 
sure,  and  the  chance*  are  that  it  is.  Chiropractic  Vertebral  Adju  i 
merits  Riven  by  a  competent  chiropractor  with  the  bare  hands  alone 
will  relieve  this  pressure.  The  effect  of  this  pressure  is  to  impede 
the  normal  flow  0f  the  Vital  force  to  ail  parts  of  the  body.  This 
then  Rives  rise  to  another  effect,  called  disease.  When  the  cause  .

Territory  is  now  proven 


Momoe  McGuire  of  this  city 
and  his  associates  have  at  last 
been  rewarded  for  their  belief  in 
the  Owsley  county  oil  field.  Last 
Wednesday  they  brought  in  their 
Peters  No.  1  whiofc  is  located  on 
Island  Creek,  near  Island  City, 
in  Owsley  county. 

This  well  was  put  on  the  pump 
Thursday  morning  early  and  has 
been  making  over  a  barrel  per 
hour  ever  since  without  the 
least  signs  of  diminishing.  As 
we  go  to  press  the  reports  from 
those  who  have  just  returned 
from  the  well  confirm  the  first 
report  that  the  well  is  good  for 
at  least  thirty-five  barrels. 

The  strike  was  made  at  a 
depth  of  735  feet  which  is  about | 
the  same  depth  of  the  Davis  Oil ! 
Company's  White  Oak  well  and 
pi oves  that  the  Davis  Oil  Com¬ 
pany's  well  and  the  Henry  wells 
on  Upper  Buffalo  are  in  the  mid¬ 
st  ol  a  great  oil  field.  The  oil 
men  are  very  optimistic  over 
•  *y-'  •vc1!  ,rtd  the  famous 

"Big  Indian"  sand  has  been  lo- 1 
cated  without  doubt.  I  his  is  the 
sand  which  has  made  West  Vir¬ 
ginia  and  Pennsylvania  so  famous 
in  the  oil  industry. 

There  is  already  quite  a  bit  of 
activity  in  the  trading  of  leases 
in  that  vicinity  and  a  rush  for 
that  field  is  now  looked  for  since 
the  territory  is  proven.

On  -last  Wednesday  at  11 
o'clock  in  the  Baptist  church  in 
Ravenna  Mrs.  R.  B.  Flanery  of 
this  city  and  David  Baker  of 
Ravenna,  were  united  in  the  holy 
Ixmds  of  wedlock.  Mr.  J.  T. 
Wallace  was  best  man  and  Mrs. 
Marv  Crawford,  of  this  city  was 
the  maid  of  honor.  Mrs  Flanery 
is  one  of  Beattvville’s  finest 
ladies,  and  Mr.  Baker  is  a  con¬ 
ductor  for  the  Louisville  and 
Nashville  Railroad  Company.  As 
soon  as  the  ceremony  was  per¬ 
formed  the  couple  left  for  Cin¬ 
cinnati  and  other  cities  on  their 
honey  moon,  after  which  they 
will  make  their  home  in  I^exing- 
ton.  The  Enterprise  ^extends  its 
congratulations.

To  The  Voters  of  Lee  County 


^  oil  now  have  the  opportunity  of  electing  as  your  of¬ 
ficers  for  the  next  four  Vears  the  following  men: 

J.  F.  Sutton  for  County  Judge 
T.  T.  Brandenburg  for  Sheriff 
E.  It.  Rose  for  County  Attorney 

*  w 

Shelby  M.  Estes  for  County  Clerk 
Robert  Brandenburg  for  Jailer 
For  Justices  of  the  Peace: 

(J.  A.  Bailey,  District  No.  I 

J 

John  Creech,' District  No.  2 
J.  K.  Hobbs,  District  No.  3 
J.  P.  Howell,  District  No.  4 

These  men  are  offered  to  the  citizens  of  Lee  County 
as  servants  to  manage  the  County's  business,  and  we  hon¬ 
estly  believe  that  no  better  men  can  be  selected  to  serve 

»  .  *  ~  *  e, 

our  people. 

These  men  are  Progressive,  Sober,  Honest,  Capable 
and  Willing  to  give  their  best  services  to  help  continue 
Lee  County's  onward  march  of  Progress. 

The  Pr<  lgressive  Platform  of  our  candidate  for  Coun¬ 
ty  Judge  is  endorsed  by  all  of  these  candidates  a-nd  they 
promise  the  people  of  Lee  County  to  stand  united  behind 
him  in  every  move  he  makes  for  the  betterment  of  our 
county. 

j  • 

We  offer  tin  *se  candidates  for  the  advancement  of 
Lee  County  and  not  for  die  purpose  of  building  up  a  po¬ 
litical  machine  for  the  benefit  of  designing  politicians. 

We  promise  the  voters  of  this  county  a  continuation 
of  our  Progressive  Road  Program  with  such  extentions  to 
all  parts  of  our  county  as  our  finances  will  permit. 

We  promise  to  lend  our  united  effort  to  the  creating  of  a  better  School  Spirit 
and  the  bringing  of  our  Educational  System  to  a  Higher  Standard  in  order  that  all  the 
Children  of  Lee  County  may  receive  the  same  advantages  for  an  Education.

To  the  Voters  of  Lee  County 


Thi.  i.  the  first  time  I  have  ever  asked  the  people  of  my  county 
to  support  me  for  an  office.  I  ask  the  support  and  vote,  of  the  cit- 
izen.  of  Lee  County  for  Sheriff  and  promise  them,  if  elected  to  thi. 
office  that  it  will  be  my  ambition  to  make  them  a  good  officer. 

I  will  endeavor  to  deal  justly  and  fairly  with  all  the  people  of  the 
county  at  all  times  and  in  a  courteous  manner. 

I  Will  make  them  a  sober  officer  and  give  to  them  a  business-like 
administration  I  will  have  only  sober,  highclas.  men  a,  my  deputies. 

I  have  the  highest  regard  for  my  opponent  and  will  not  use  any 
unfair  mean,  to  bring  about  his  defeat.  I  am  running  upon  my  own 
record  as  a  citizen  and  invite  all  those  who  do  not  know  me  person- 

ally  to  inquire  about  me.  I  will  work  and  use  my  influence  tor  a  bet- 
ter  and  more  prosperous  county. 

Your  support  is  earnestly  solicited. 


T.  T.  Brandenburg

The  democratic  candidates  and  some  of  their  friends  have  raised  a  great  hubbub 
about  their  failure  to  file  their  certificates  of  nomination  fortv-five  davs  before  the  election. 
Kor  many  years  and  until  the  law  w  is  changed  bv  the  legislature  in  1918,  these  certificates 
were  required  to  be  filed  not  less  than  fifteen  davs  before  the  general  election.  In  that  same 
vear  the  law  was  so  changed  as  to  require  them  to  be  filed  not  less  than  fortv-five  davs  l»e- 


fore  the  election  and  this  has  since  been  the  law 


Only  one  democratic  candidate  complied  with  the  law.  towit:  Mr.  Robert  Bran¬ 
denburg.  ctndidate  for  Jailer,  wh  >  filed  his  certificate  on  September  7,  1925.  Mr.  Branden¬ 
burg  is  not  a  lawyer,  yet  he  did  wha>  the  law  requires.  Two  of  the  democratic  candidates 


are  lawyers;  yet  they  and  the  other  d  n  >  ratic  candidates  failed  to  tile  in  time  as  Mr.  Bran- 


denburg  did.  altho  this  law  .s  in  the  Statute  tor  anybody  to  read.  Those  who  seek  informa¬ 
tion  on  this  point  should  inquire  of  the  democratic  lawyer  candidates  who  doubtless  can  give 
detailed  information. 


Apparently  the  failure  to  file  in  time  was  not  noticed  unfit  a  similar  question 
came  up  in  Estill  county,  and  then  the  democratic  candidates  who  had  not  filed  in  time  filed 
their  certificates  with  the  county  clerk  Mr.  W.  ()  Bradley,  who  stated  that  he  would  put 
them  on  the  ballot,  notwithstanding  their  failure  to  file  in  time.  Matters  stood  thus  until  the 
Court  of  Appeals  decided  that  the  democratic  candidates  in  Estill  county  could  not  go  on  the 
ballot  and  then  all  the  democratic  andidatesin  Lee.  except  Mr.  Robert  Brandenburg  and  Mr 
E.  B.  Rose,  resigned  their  nominal  ors  and  the  democ  ratic  committee  named  other  candi¬ 
dates  in  their  place 


The  new  candidates  t  ien  filed  their  certificates  of  nomination  with  the  county 
clerk  and  brought  suit  io  have  their  names  placed  on  the  ballot.  The  Court  of  Appeals  di- 
rected  the  names  of  the  new’  candidates  to  be  placed  on  the  ballot  and  then  the  new  candi¬ 
dates  resigned  their  nominations  i  id  the  democratic  committee  renominated  the  old  nomi¬ 
nees  and  Mr.  Bradley,  the  county  clerk,  is  placing  the  names  of  the  old  nominees  on  the  bal¬ 
lot  Three  republican  candidates  tor  magistrate,  one  lor  constable  and  one  tor  County  Sur¬ 
veyor,  failed  to  hie  in  tune,  in  th*-  ^aine  manner  as  the  democrats  did  and  their  names  are  be¬ 
ing  placed  on  the  ballot,  as  the  have  been  renominated  by  the  republican  committee 


Neither  the  republics  i  candidates  nor  the  repbulican  committee  nor  \V.  O.  Brad 
ley  have  ever  tried  to  keep  any  democratic  nominee  off  the  ballot.  The  printing  of  the  bal- 
lots  has  been  held  up  for  the  express  purpose  Af  allowing  the  democrats  to  make  all  the 
changes  in  their  candidates  that  .hev  desire  to  make  and  now  that  they  have  finally  got  their 
old  candidates  on  the  ticket,  the  ballots  are  being  printed  just  as  the  democratic  committee 
desires.  The  republican  candid. itei  have  always  been  w  illing  and  are  now  willing  to  run 
against  any  candidates  the  democratic  party  might  name. 

We  appeal  to  you  to  vote  for  th- republican  candidates.

KENTUCKY,  FLAG,  FLOWER,  BIRD  AND  SONG 


Frequently  we  are  asked:  “Has  Kentucky  a  State  Flag?” 
or,  “What  is  the  State  Flower  of  Kentucky?”  The  State  flower 
is  the  goldenrod-  The  native  red  bird  or  Kentucky  Cardinal, 
is  the  State  bird  and  "My  Old  Kentucky  Home”  written  by 
Stephen  Colline  Foster  is  the  official  state  song.  The  State 
Flag  is  of  silk  or  bunting,  navy  blue,  with  the  seal  of  the  Com¬ 
monwealth  encircled  by  a  wreath  qf  goldenrod  in  the  center. 
The  seal  of  the  State  and  the  wreath  may  be  embroidered, 
printed  or  stamped  and  the  dimensions  of  the  flag  may  vary.

BEATTYVILLE,  LEE  COUNTY,  KENTUCKY  Friday,  March  25  ,  1932 


NO.  13 


McKinley  Rice  Fatally 
Shot  Saturday  Evening 


The  entire  set  of  officials  in 
the  oral  finals  of  The  Courier- 
Journal  State  Spelling  Bee  will  be 
educators  experienced  in  such 
competitions.  Misses  Ruby  M. 
Conkling  of  Louisville  Girls  High 
School  and  Nelda  Waterman  of 
Louisville  Normal  School  both 
have  served  as  pronouncers  in 
previous  State  matches.  The 
judges  are  H.  J.  Hainfan,  super¬ 
intendent  ot  Puducah  City  Schools 
Dean  Cloyd  McAllister  of  Berea 
College,  and  Father  George  R. 
Satlm,  superintendent  of  parochi¬ 
al  schools  of  the  Louisville  Dio¬ 
cese.  While  bather  Saffin  is 
serving  lor  the  lirst  time  in  a 
State  match,  he  formerly  was 
assigned  to  8t.  Alary’s  parish  in 
Paducah,  v>  hich  produced  three 
State  champion  spellers,  and  he 
is  familiar  with  the  procedure  in 
Spelling  Bees. 

Spellers  competing  in  the  State 
match  this  year  will  meet  at 
9  o  clock,  Wednesday  morning, 
April  13,  in  the  auditorium  of 
Louisville  Male  High  School. 
Brook  and  Breckinridge  Streets, 
to  take  a  written  test  of  fifty 
words.  The  names  of  the  twenty! 
to  forty  spellers  who  make  the  i 
highest  grades  in  this  written, 
test  will  oe  announced  at  a  lunch¬ 
eon  to  be  given  by  The  Courier- 
Journal  in  honor  of  spellers  at 
noon  at  the  Kentucky  Hotel,  and 
these  spellers  will  enter  the  oral 
competition  in  tne  atternoon. 

T  he  Slate  Spelling  Bee  is  the 
opening  leature  of  the  Kentucky 
Lducalioj  Association  convention 
and  its  officers  and  directors  will  i 
be  invited  to  attend  the  luncheon 
in  honor  of  spellers.  Announce¬ 
ment  of  the  hour  WHAS  radio¬ 
phone  of  The  Courier- Journal 
and  ihe  limes,  will  raidocast 
the  final  match  probably  will  be 
announced  within  the  next  week. 

Floreine  Marcum,  of  Belle 
Point  will  represent  Lee  Countv 
in  the  state  match  as  decided  by 
the  county  bee  sponsored  by  The 
Enterprise  and  the  county  super¬ 
intendent,  Sam  B.  Taylor,  and 1 
held  here  February  13. 


McK'nley  Rice,  who  at  one 
rime  was  an  employee  in  the 
postoffice  here,  but  moved  away 
some  ten  or  twelve  years  ago, 
was  shot  and  fatally  wounded  on 
Tipton  Ridge  in  Estill  county, 
about  7:30  in  the  evening  of 
March  19. 

Kice  was  returning  to  his  home , 
in  Irvine  at  the  time  the  shooting 
occurred  and  stopped  at  a  filling 
station  to  purchase  cigarettes 
when  he  was  accosted  by  Don¬ 
ald  Barnes,  23,  of  Furnace,  Es- 
till  county,  whom  it  is  reported 
that  he  arrested  several  years 
ago  a  charge  of  attempted  as¬ 
sault  on  a  woman.  It  is  alleged 
that  Barnes  reminded  Rice  of 
the  arrest  and  opened  fire  with 
i  two  pistols,  the  first  shot  struck 
Kice  in  the  chest  and  passed  en¬ 
tirely  through  his  body.  Rice 
I  turned  to  run  and  was  struck  by 
two  more  bullets,  one  in  the  back 
and  one  in  the  arm.  It  is  said 
that  either  of  the  two  shots  in 
the  body  would  have  been  fatal. 
Rice  got  to  the  car  with  his  com¬ 
panions  who  immediately  start-  , 
ed  to  Irvine  but  Rice  was  dead 
before  they  reached  that  city. 

Barnes  was  arrested  several  , 
hours  later  by  Estill  county  offic¬ 
ers  and  when  brought  before 
County  Judge  Harry  B.  Wilson 
examining  trial  was  set  for  Wed-  ' 
nesday  morning.  Barnes  was 
found  guilty  at  the  examining 
trial  and  bound  over  to  the  next 
session  of  the  grand  jury  with¬ 
out  bail.

Zachari-shyRoad  Likely  To 
Be  Completed  In  Six  Weeks 


Work  on  the  Zachariah  road 
was  started  several  days  ago  and 
if  good  weather  continues  it  is 
believed  that  work  of  grading 
will  be  finished  by  the  last  of 
thid  week.  According  to  a  state¬ 
ment  from  one  of  the  engineers 
in  charge  of  the  work  the  entire 
job  can  be  completed  in  about 
six  weeks  if  weather  will  permit 
work  for  the  major  part  of  that 
time.

The  Lee  County  Health  De¬ 
partment  this  week  completed 
inspections  covering  the  sixth, 
seventh  and  eighth  grades  of  the 
St  Helens  school  in  connection 
with  the  Blue  Ribbon  Contest, 
awards  to  winners  of  which  will 
be  made  on  May  Day.  Pupils  of 
other  schools  in  the  county  have 
already  been  inspected.  The 
inspections  deal  with  physical 
fitness  and  are  to  promote  bet¬ 
ter  health  among  the  children 
of  school  age.  Where  imperfec¬ 
tions  were  found  the  pupils  were 
advised  how  they  might  be  cor¬ 
rected. 

An  official  proclamation  by 
President  Hoover  was  issued  this 
week  naming  May  Day  as  Na¬ 
tional  Child  Health  day.  Win¬ 
ners  in  these  contests  which  cov¬ 
er  the  entire  nation  will  be 
awarded  a  blue  ribbon  bearing  a 
suitable  inscription  at  that  time. 


A  story  in  the  issue  of  March 
23  of  the  Louisville  Courier-Jour¬ 
nal  states  that  fifty-two  boot¬ 
leggers  held  a  convention  on  Tip- 
ton  Ridge  in  Estill  County  on 
March  22  and  rendered  an  ultima- 
tion  that  they  would  not  support 
any  candidate  for  office  that 
would  prosecute  them  for  their 
activities. 

Many  of  the  men  who  are  said 
to  have  attended  the  convention, 
the  first  of  its  kind  ever  held  in 
Estill  County,  said  that  bootleg¬ 
ging  was  the  only  means  that 
they  had  of  supporting  their 
fam  i  lies. 

The  ultimation  was  directed  to 
canmdates  for  county  offices  and 
expressed  in  a  resolution  adopted 
by  the  convention.  The  chair- 
mai  announced  that  at  the  next 
me.  ting  bootleggers  from  other 
countie*  would  convene  with 
th«m.

Kentucky  Farm  New* 


Thousands  of  acres  of  poor  land 
in  Fulton  county  will  he  sowed 
to  grass  mixtures  and  clovers, 
according  to  County  Agent  H.  C. 
Brown.  Much  land  that  has 
been  devoted  to  soybeans  will  be 
sowed  to  lespedeza.  Grass  mix¬ 
tures  are  being  sowed  in  many 
!  lespedeza  fields.  The  county 
ajrent  is  endeavoring  to  have 
farmers  produce  enough  feed  for 
their  livestock. 

For  the  second  time  in  the 
testing  year,  a  herd  owned  by 
Clav  S.  Johnson  in  Oldham  coun¬ 
ty  last  month  made  the  highest 
producing  average  in  the  Oldham 
Jefferson  Dairy  Herd  Improve¬ 
ment  Association.  His  herd 
averaged  1.092  pounds  of  milk 
and  36  pounds  of  butterfat  per 
cow.  He  is  feeding  alfalfa  hav 
and  crushed  corn.  R.  L.  Duncan 
another  ( )ldham  county  farmer 
owned  the  second  best  herd.

Talk"  has  been  issued  by  the 
poultry  department  of  the  Col¬ 
lege  of  Agriculture,  University 
of  Kentucky,  for  the  benefit  of 
persons  interested  in  raising 
turkeys.  It  discusses  the  selec-

Loan  Applicants 

Must  Grow  Garden 


Farmers  obtaining  government 
loans  to  assist  them  in  putting 
out  this  year’s  crops  must  agree 
to  grow  a  garden  for  home  use 
and  a  sufficient  acreage  of  feed 
crops  to  supply  feed  for  their 
livestock,  it  is  pointed  out  by 
Dean  Thomas  P.  Cooper  of  the 
College  of  Agriculture,  Universi¬ 
ty  of  Kentucky.  This  conforms 
with  the  live-at-home  program

Somerset.  Ky.— The  combined 
years  of  military  service  repre¬ 
sented  in  the  color  guard  of 
Pulaski  Post  No.  38.  the  Ameri¬ 
can  Legion,  this  city,  are  100 
years,  according  to  Dr.  A.  B. 
Waddle,  post  adjutant.  Three 
members  of  the  guard,  Martin 
Kvarnes,  Sam  Ramsey  and  Chas. 
Ramsey,  have  each  served  30 
years.  The  fourth  member, 
Dean  Lynch,  has  a  record  of  10 
years’  service.  Another  member 
of  the  Post.  Will  Gossett,  will  be 
added  to  the  color  guard.  He 
has  served  30  years’  military 
service. 

Calhoun.  Ky, — Under  the  aus¬ 
pices  of  James  Bethel  Gresham 
Post  No.  60,  American  Legion, 
i  this  city,  a  series  of  diphtheria 
clinics  were  held  throughout 
McLean  county  during  January 
and  February.  At  one  point, 
more  than  100  children  were  im¬ 
munized.  The  program  was  in 
1  charge  of  Post  Commander  Ed. 
G.  Linderman  and  Post  Adjutant 
D.  C.  Ross. 

Safety  first  work  is  fast  be¬ 
coming  one  of  the  major  activi¬ 
ties  of  the  American  Legion. 

Scott  County  Post  No.  24, 
Georgetown  gave  a  St.  Patrick 
day  dance. 

Douglas  Laws  Post  No.  52, 
Harrodsburg,  is  having  every 
student  in  Mercer  County  write 
an  essay  on  George  Washington. 
A  big  all  day  patriotic  rally  will 
be  held  at  Harrodsburg  in  April, 
when  the  prize  winning  essays 
are  read  and  the  prize  awarded.

Auxiliary,  announced  that  she 
has  organized  a  Unit  of  the  Auxi¬ 
liary  for  Nicholas  Post  No.  17, 
American  Legion,  Carlisle.

Syphilis  and  other  infections, 
it  fid-poisoning.  overeating,  ex¬ 
it  ssive  muscular  activity,  and  a

alcohtdland  tobacco  in  the  list. 

Bodily  and  mental  fatigue,  diz¬ 
ziness,  i  headache,  ringing  in  the 
ears,  and  numbness  and  cramps 
are  early  symptons.  All  of  these 
may  be  caused  by  other  condi¬ 
tions.  so  are  not  peculiar  to 
arteriosclerosis.

Detroit,  Mich.  A  special  ser¬ 
vice  campaign  of  interest  to  the 
more  than  4.(Mk).«MK)  Chevrolet 
owners  in  the  United  States  is  to 
ho  launched  on  a  national  scale 
by  (  hevrolet  dealers  beginning 
Mar.  28,  M.  D.  Douglas,  general 
parts  and  service  manager  of  the 
Chevrolet  Motor  Company,  an¬ 
nounced  here  today. 

Designed  to  improve  the  oper¬ 
ation  and  appearance  of  cars  now 
in  owners'  hands,  the  campaign 
will  have  the  further  effect  of 
adding  to  the  safety  of  the  high¬ 
ways  by  insuring  against  any 
possible  mechanical  defects  in 
those  cars  serviced  during  the 
campaign,  Mr.  Douglas  pointed 
out. 

Some  vital  operation,  ranging 
from  brake  adjustments  to  wheel 
alignment  and  complete  tighten¬ 
ing  of  every  part  of  the  chassis, 
is  to  be  offered  by  the  dealers  at 
sizable  reductions  from  the  flat 
rate  charge,  for  a  period  of  one 
week  only  during  the  campaign. 
Ten  such  featured  services,  ail 
having  to  do  with  appearance 
and  performance,  are  in  the  cam¬ 
paign,  which  extends  over  a  per¬ 
iod  of  ten  weeks. 

National  newspaper  advertis¬ 
ing,  as  well  as  special  newspaper 
campaigns  for  the  dealer’s  local 
use,  are  to  puDlicize  the  event 
and  the  featured  services  each 
week.  Radio  announcements  on 
a  national  and  local  scale  also  are 
to  be  used. 

Mr.  Douglas  pointed  out  that 
the  special  rates  during  the  cam¬ 
paign  will  induce  many  people, 
who  otherwise  might  not  have 
their  car  serviced,  to  check  at 
least  vital  points  pertaining  to 
performance  before  they  get  out 
on  the  highways  d  u  ring  the 
heavy  motoring  season. 


in  state  institutions. 

Newport.  Ky.—  A  gold  life 
membership  plate,  the  first  of 
its  kind  ever  to  be  given  a  Ken¬ 
tucky  legionnaire,  was  presented 
to  Harper  Moore,  former  finance 
officer  of  James  Wallace  Costigan 
Post  No.  11,  American  Legion, 
this  city,  Thursday  night.  March 
10,  w  hen  over  300  members  of 
the  post  gathered  at  legion  hall. 

Moore  was  one  of  the  founders 
of  the  Costigan  post,  which  was 
formed  in  September.  1019,  and 
has  been  an  officer  of  the  organi¬ 
zation  continuously  since  that 
time.  However,  his  work  re¬

Louisville.  Ky.—  The  War 
Against  Depression,  or  the 
United  Action  Campaign,  being 
sponsored  by  the  American  Le¬ 
gion.  in  cooperation  with  the 
American  Federation  of  Labor, 
the  Association  of  National  Ad¬ 
vertisers  and  the  American  Le¬ 
gion  Auxiliary,  to  place  a  mil¬ 
lion  unemployed  persons  hack  to 
work,  has  averaged  approximate¬ 
ly  10,000  jobs  a  day  since  the 
drive  was  inaugurated  in  Febru¬ 
ary.  Starting  with  less  than 
ona  hundred  communities,  more 
than  1500  are  now  organized  and 
actively  engaged  in  the  War. 

The  Louisville  sector  is  now 
the  scene  of  a  “big  push.’’  which 
started  Thursday.  March  17,  and 
will  continue  until  3000  men  are 
placed  in  jobs  and  $3,000.00  in 
new  contracts  for  construction  or 
repair  work  are  secured.  Nearly 
1000  members  of  Jefferson  Post 
No.  15,  American  Legion,  and 
the  American  Federation  of 
Labor,  took  to  the  field  in  open 
war;  are,  Saturday  morning, 
March  19.  Brig.  General  Ellerbe 
W.  Carter  is  in  command  of  the 
Louisville  sector  and  has  all  civic, 
religious,  trade  and  commercial 
groups  of  the  city  cooperating. 

Department  Commander  Bacon 
R.  Moore,  Harrodsburg,  again 
announced  that  the  War  should 
be  carried  to  every  county  of  the 
state  if  it  took  all  year  and  tnat 
the  Legion  expected  to  keep  up 
the  campaign  until  the  land  of 
good  times  had  been  recaptured 
from  the  actrocious  forces  of  de¬ 
pression.

Hardening  of  the  Arteries 

Hardening  of  the  arteries  is 
called  arteriosclerosis,  It  is  one 
of  the  most  important  features 
of  the  process  of  growing  old. 
Hence,  the  saying  that  "a  man 
is  as  old  as  his  arteries.’’

This  country  needs  and  lacks  ade¬ 
quate  economical  distribution.  For 
that  reason  It  Is  folly  to  tell  four  of 
the  greatest  industrial  concerns  In 
the  United  States,  now  engaged  la 
distribution  of  the  farmers'  cattle, 
pigs  and  chickens,  that  they  must  not 
distribute  other  products  of  the  farms. 

To  forbid  full  use  of  the  packers’ 

distributing  agencies  Is  like  telling  a 
milkman  that  he  can  sell  only  milk, 
another  wagon  must  bring  the  cream, 
and  a  third  the  buttermilk. 

The  consuming  public  at  one  end. 
the  farmer  at  the  other  pay  all  the 
distribution  costs. 

Those  that  distribute  meat  should 
distribute  all  farm  products — fruits 
vegetables,  raw  and  canned— every 
thing,  in  short,  that  they  can  die 
tribute,  everything  that  the  farm  pro 
duces  and  the  consumer  wants.  To 
limit  or  forbid  the  business  tnan  the 
right  to  do  what  he  choose*  In  his 
own  wav  In  legitimate  business  u

Erosion 


By  Ethel  Brandenburg 


A  survey  in  one  of  the  South- 
f-  States  showed  that  one  coun¬ 
ty  ontained  90.000  acres  of  for- 
rio  r|y  cultivated  land  which  has 
now  been  permanently  ruined  by 
'■rt  Mon  due  to  excessive  washing 
and  gullying,  even  down  to  the 
bedrock  in  many  cases.  A  sur¬ 
vey  of  another  county  in  tie 
>arne  area  shows  that  297.000 
acr<i§,  or  50per  cent  of  the  total 
area,  has  largely  lost  its  surface 
soil  since  the  land  was  cleared.

PARAGRAPHS  FROM 

The  Lniyersity  of  Kentucky 


Social  Fraternities 

Initiate  Members 


Approximately  118  young  men 
and  women  students  at  the  Uni¬ 
versity  of  Kentucky  were  initiat¬ 
ed  into  membership  into  social 
fraternities  during  the  past  week. 
Of  this  number  92  were  men  and 
26  were  women  students.  Re¬ 
quirements  for  membership  are 
a  scholastic  standing  as  is  requir¬ 
ed  by  the  University  and  one  se¬ 
mester  on  the  campus  previous 
to  initiation.  One  Beattyville 
boy  was  initiated.  Homer  Bran¬ 
denburg.  Alpha  Tau  Omega. 


An  article  by  Dr.  Paul  P.  Boyd 
dean  of  the  College  of  Arts  and 
Sciences  at  the  University  of 
Kentucky,  entitled  “fhanging 
the  Emphasis  from  Quantitative 
to  Qualitative  Standards  in  Ac¬ 
crediting  of  High  Schools.’*  was 
recently  published  in  the  March 
issue  of  the  North  Central  As¬ 
sociation  Quarterly.  Thi-  paper, 
which  Dean  Boyd  read  before 
the  officers  of  the  association  in 
Detroit,  accredition  that  will 
measure  more  accurately  the 
essential  quality  of  the  work 
done  in  the  schools,  and  sug¬ 
gests  various  changes  that  should 
be  made  in  the  standards. 

Dr.  Frank  L.  McVey,  president 
of  the  University  of  Kentucky, 
addressed  the  Southern  division 
of  the  American  Mining  Con¬ 
gress  at  its  annual  dinn-  r  meet¬ 
ing  held  March  17  in  L  >uisville. 
His  subject  was  “The  Relation 
of  Education  to  Industry.”

Smaller  Turkeys 


Let  Your  Easter 
Greeting  Be 


The  turkeys  which  grace 
Thanksgiving  and  Christmas 
boards  are  gradually  growing 
smaller,  says  H.  L.  Shrader, 
poultry  husbandman  of  the  Unit¬ 
ed  States  Department  of  Agri¬ 
culture.  Ten  years  ago  consum¬ 
ers  preferred  large  toms  and  the 
smaller  birds  (usually  hens) 
brought  lower  prices,  but  now 
the  market  demand  is  for  birds 
weighing  an  average  of  10  pounds 
dres-ed.  This  year  the  smaller 
turkeys  have  commanded  a  prem¬ 
ium  of  3  or  4  cents  a  pound.  Mr. 
Shrader  says.  One  of  the  larg¬ 
est  chain-store  buyers  states  that 
three-fourths  of  the  demand  in 
his  stores  is  for  birds  weighing 
from  8  to  12  pounds.

Evening  services  at  7:00  p.m. 

Everybody  cordially  invited  to 
attend.

Who  makes  your 
Fire  Insurance  Rate? 


Reported  By  M.  C.  Newnam 

Clay  bunigan  and  Miss  Ladon- 
na  Newnam  were  quietly  mar¬ 
ried  last  week. 

Bill  Dan  Dunaway  and  Miss 
Gale  Dunigan  were  quitely  mar¬ 
ried  Saturday  night. 

T.  C.  Brandenburg  was  calling 


Property  owners — individually  and  collectively — create  the 
conditions  which  determine  the  cost  cf  their  fire  insurance. 

There  is  nothing  secret  in  the  making  of  fire  insurance  rates. 
Several  major  facts  enter  into  the  determination  of  these  charges, 
such  as  construction,  occupancy,  th<  quality  of  private  and 
public  fire  protection,  exposure  from  other  property  and  general 
loss  experience. 

ELIMINATE  HAZARDS 

Stock  Fire  Insurance  Companies  maintain  bureaus  to  assist  in 
eliminating  fire  hazards  or  correcting  defects  which  may  affect 
your  nuc.  The  advice  of  such  bureaus  is  offered  to  you  free  of 
charge  through  your  insurance  agent.

Daviess  county  reports  in¬ 
creased  interest  in  growing  bur- 
ley  tobacco.  Forty-three  farm¬ 
ers  have  secured  seed  from  the 
Experiment  Station. 

Pulaski  county  farmers  are 
milking  more  cows  since  the 
f*?tablishment  of  a  milk  plant  at 
Somerset. 

Pasture  improvement  and  the 
production  of  more  feed  are  the 
major  projects  in  every  com¬ 
munity  program  in  Elliott  county. 

Grass  and  pasture  mixtures, 
legumes,  road  improvement, 
gardens,  poultry  and  soil  erosion 
are  included  in  13  community 
programs  in  Rockcastle  county. 

Flocks  fed  home-mixed  rations 
made  the  best  record  in  Christian 
county  egg-laying  contest  last 
month. 

The  state  veterinary  depart¬ 
ment  is  planning  to  test  Knott 
county  cattle  for  tuberculosis 
during  June. 

Graves  county  farmers  have 
asked  for  demonstrations  in  fir- 
ing  their  1932  tobacco  crop. _

Seven  Departments 
Over  The  Top  In 
Legion  Membership 


Indianapolis,  Ind.-- Seven  de¬ 
partments  of  the  American  Le¬ 
gion  are  now  over  the  top  in  1932 
membership,  according  to  records 
of  the  national  treasurer  here. 
Nevada  exceeded  its  quota  for 
the  year  on  March  7th,  Michigan 
on  March  9th.  West  Virginia  on 
March  lbth,  and  the  department 
of  France  on  March  14th.  Other 
departments  already  over  the  top 
in  the  order  named  are  Hawaii, 
Rhode  Island  and  Maryland. 


Twenty  home-made  brick 
brooder  stoves  are  being  used  in 
Barren  county.

81st  Division  Reunion 


Five  Harrison  county  4-H  club 
boys  have  purchased  Angus  cows 
with  calves  at  side 


Former  members  of  the  81st 
"Wildcat”  Division  have  been  re¬ 
quited  by  Staff  Sergeant  James 
E  Cahall,  2012  Portner  Place, 
N  II  Washington  D.  C.,  to 

communicate  with  hirn  in  regard 

to  plans  for  a 
ing  to  f'ahall,

as  founda¬ 
tions  for  herds  of  purebred  beef 
cattle.  They  are  George  Midden, 
James  Gray,  Robert  Haviland, 
Joe  Marsh  and  Joseph  Midden. 
The  calves  will  be  fattened  for 
the  fat  cattle  show  and  sale  at 
the  Bourbon  Stock  Yards  in  Louis¬ 
ville,  and  the  cows  kept  and 
breeding  herds  built  up. 

The  development  of  breeding 
herds  by  farm  boys  is  a  part  of 
the  4-H  club  educational  work  of 
the  College  of  Agriculture.  Uni¬ 
versity  of  Kentucky.  Under  the 
direction  of  county  agents  and 
field  agents  from  the  College, 
outstanding  farms  boys  are 
taught  the  fundamental  principles 
of  good  livestock  breeding. 

Forty-five  Herelord  calves, 
purchased  in  Kansas  City,  Mo., 
have  been  distributed  to 28  Mont¬ 
gomery  county  4-H  club  boys  and 
girls.  They  weighed  approxi¬ 
mately  500  pounds  and  cost  an 
average  of  (37.50  at  Mt  Sterling. 
Montgomery  county  4-H  club 
members  also  are  finishing  15 
Angus  calves  for  the  fat  cattle 
show  and  sale  at  the  Bourbon 
Stock  Yards.

The  electric  light  and  power 
companies  of  the  nation  paid 
$16,000,000,  or  8.5  per  cent, 
more  in  taxes  in  1930  than  in 
1929.  In  a  period  of  29  years, 
from  1902  to  1930,  inclusive,  the 
taxes  paid  per  dollar  of  gross 
revenue  have  trebled. 

Whether  or  not  these  taxes  are 
excessive  or  equitable  is  beside 
the  point  here.  What  is  not  be¬ 
side  the  point  is  th*  tremendous 
contibution  made  by  the  power 
companies  to  the  upkeep  of 
government.  During  1930,  as  in 
1931.  most  businesses  were  more 
or  less  in  the  doldru  s.  Their , 
tax  payments  fell  off  critically,  ’ 
as  the  Treasury  records  show.

Sugar  and  sweets  of  the  candy 
kind  may  also  have  a  place  in 
the  diet.  But  certainly  not  in 
excessive  quanites.  Fruits  and 
some  variety  of  vegetables  should 
have  an  important  place.  Vege¬ 
tables  contain  starches,  sugar 
and  proteins  besides  mineral 
salts  and  vitamins.  They  are 
not  only  healthful  and  valuable 
foods,  but  often  leave  some  fib¬ 
rous  residue  in  the  intestine. 
This  favors  a  normal  action  of 
the  bowels  and  helps  prevent 
constipation.

Details  of  the  great  radio  meet¬ 
ing  are  being  worked  out.  The 
broadcast  will  constitute  one-half 
of  the  program.  The  first  forty- 
five  minutes  of  the  broadcast 
will  be  over  the  N.  B.  C.  "red” 
network,  and  the  last  fifteen 
minutes  over  both  the  "red”  and 
the  "blue”  coast-to-coast  N.  B.  C. 
network.  One  hundred  and 
forty-six  posts  in  Kentucky  will 
be  in  public  meeting  assembled 
at  the  same  time. 

Under  the  plan  outlined  by 
Henry  L.  Stevens.  Jr.,  national 


Depression  Campaign  in  Ken¬ 
tucky.  stated  that  he  was  well 
pleased  with  the  progress  made 
by  posts  in  those  communities 
that  had  organized  and  conduct¬ 
ing  a  militant  campaign.  He 
cited  the  results  being  obtained 
in  Louisville  where  the  Campaign 
is  just  now  well  under  way.  2360 
men  having  been  returned  to 
gainful  employment  and  over 
one  million  dollars  pledged  for 
new  construction  and  repair 
work. 

At  Lexington,  the  campaign  is 
under  the  direct  supervision  of 
the  Chamber  of  Commerce,  as¬ 
sisted  bv  the  Legion,  and  is  ob¬ 
taining  splendid  results. 

Harry  M.  Byllesby,  Ashland. 
Commander  of  Clarence  Fields 
Post  No.  76,  American  Legion, 
is  leading  a  successful  offensive 
in  that  city.  Owensboro.  Rus¬ 
sellville,  Paducah.  Bowling 
Green,  Newport,  Marion.  May- 
field.  Princeton,  Louisa  and 
Somerset  are  also  carrying  on 
the  Depression  War  in  a  most 
energetic  manner,  while  some 
forty  additional  posts  located  in 
communities  where  unemploy¬ 
ment  is  not  such  a  critical  prob¬ 
lem,  are  actively  engaged  in 
every  possible  relief  and  business 
stimulating  et  forts.

by  T.  H.  Hayden,  Jr.,  depart¬ 
ment  adjutant,  each  post  is  to 
have  a  guest  speaker,  from  some 
other  city  or  community,  to  ad¬ 
dress  it  as  a  part  of  the  program. 

Louisville.  Ky.—  During  the 
month  of  April,  American  Legion 
Posts  throughout  Kentucky  have 
been  requested  to  hold  meetings 
for  the  study  and  promotion  of 
better  international  relations.  A 
notice  has  been  sent  to  all  post 
commanders  by  Hart  G.  Foster. 
Lexington,  chairman  of  Ken¬ 
tucky  Department’s  Committee 
on  Foreign  Relations,  designating 
April  as  the  month  for  this  pur¬ 
pose.  This  is  in  accordance  with 
a  mandate  of  the  last  National 
Convention  that  a  certain  period 
be  set  asida  each  year  for  posts 
to  give  their  attention  and  con¬ 
sideration  to  the  study  of  inter¬ 
national  relations. 

Danville,  Ky.  —  J.  Thompson 
Crooks,  of  this  city,  Americanism 
Chairman  of  the  American  Le¬ 
gion,  Department  of  Kentucky, 
is  preparing  an  appeal  to  every 
post  of  the  organization  in  Ken¬ 
tucky  to  enter  a  team  in  the  Le¬ 
gion’s  1932  Junior  Baseball  pro¬ 
gram.  Last  year  half  a  million 
boys  participated  in  this  great 
feature  of  the  Legion’s  American¬ 
ism  program,  Kentucky  having 
approximately  150  teams.  Chair- 


ment  commander,  was  the  prin¬ 
cipal  speaker. 

Bourbon  Post  No.  30,  American 
Legion,  Paris,  entertained  Scott 
County  Post  No.  24,  George¬ 
town,  with  a  banquet  at  the 
Windsor  Hotel,  March  22.  The 
Georgetown  outfit  won  the  free 
banquet  in  a  membership  contest 
with  the  Legionnaires.

In  the  early  part  of  the  garden 
Mrs.  Harting  grows  peas,  beans, 
potatoes,  lima  beans,  early  toma¬ 
toes,  cabbage,  okra,  early  cucum¬ 
bers,  parsnips,  carrots  and  sal¬ 
sify.  Rhubarb  is  put  near  the 
border,  where  it  is  least  disturb¬ 
ed  and  yet  can  be  cultivated. 
Next  to  the  rhubarb,  and  be- 
I  tween  the  early  and  late  gar¬ 
dens,  a  space  of  10  feet  is  lelt 
for  flowers. 

The  late  garden  contains  beans 
and  tomatoes  for  canning,  beans 
and  sweet  potatoes  for  winter} 
storing,  and  cucumbers,  sweet 
pepper  and  hot  pepper,  and  also 
peanuts,  popcorn  for  winter,  and 
watermelons  and  muskmelons. 
By  planting  green  beans  every 
two  or  three  weeks  Mrs.  Harting 
has  beans  all  snmmer  until  frost 
and  a  sufficient  supply  for  can- ! 
ning.

Asks  That  Unused 

Wards  Be  Opened 

Washington,  D.  C.  —  In  view 
of  the  scarcity  of  hospital  facili¬ 
ties  for  the  treatment  of  veter¬ 
ans  suffering  from  tuberculosis, 
the  American  Legion  is  urging 
the  Veterans’  Administration  to 
arrange  for  the  reopening  of 
three  hitherto  unused  wards  at 
the  Veterans’  Hospital,  Whipple, 
Arizona.  It  is  pointed  out  that 
this,  with  the  appointment  of  a 
limited  number  of  medical  and 
other  personnel,  would  make 
available  about  150  beds  almost 
immediately.  From  the  last  sta¬ 
tistics  received  in  the  Legion’s 
rehabilitation  office  here,  about 
100  veterans  are  on  the  waiting 
list  for  admission  to  hospitaliza¬ 
tion  in  the  of  Arizona  alone.
